=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
For a positive rational number x, define f(x) as follows:

Express x as \dfrac{P}{Q} using coprime positive integers P and Q. f(x) is defined as the value P\times Q.

You are given a positive integer N and a sequence A=(A_1,A_2,\dots,A_{N-1}) of positive integers of length N-1.
We call a sequence S=(S_1,S_2,\dots,S_N) of positive integers of length N a good sequence if it satisfies all of the following conditions:

- For every integer i with 1\leq i\leq N-1, it holds that f\left(\dfrac{S_i}{S_{i+1}}\right)=A_i.
- \gcd(S_1,S_2,\dots,S_N)=1.

Define the score of a sequence as the product of all its elements.
It can be proved that there are finitely many good sequences. Find the sum, modulo 998244353, of the scores of all good sequences.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{N-1}

Output

Print the sum, modulo 998244353, of the scores of all good sequences.

Constraints


- 2\leq N\leq 1000
- 1\leq A_i\leq 1000 (1\leq i\leq N-1)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
1 9 2 2 9

Sample Output 1

939634344

For example, both (2,2,18,9,18,2) and (18,18,2,1,2,18) are good sequences, and both have a score of 23328.
There are a total of 16 good sequences, and the sum of the scores of all of them is 939634344.

Sample Input 2

2
9

Sample Output 2

18

There are 2 good sequences, both with a score of 9.

Sample Input 3

25
222 299 229 22 999 922 99 992 22 292 222 229 992 922 22 992 222 222 99 29 92 999 2 29

Sample Output 3

192457116

Do not forget to compute the sum modulo 998244353.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo for solve_prime(alphas, p):
    H = sum(alphas)
    if H == 0: return 1
    powq = [1]*(H+1)
    q = p % MOD
    for i in range(1,H+1): powq[i] = powq[i-1]*q % MOD
    dp0 = [0]*(H+1)
    dp1 = [0]*(H+1)
    for h in range(H+1):
        w = powq[h]
        if h == 0:
            dp1[h] = w
        else:
            dp0[h] = w
    for alpha in alphas:
        ndp0 = [0]*(H+1)
        ndp1 = [0]*(H+1)
        if alpha == 0:
            for h in range(H+1):
                w0 = dp0[h]
                w1 = dp1[h]
                if w0:
                    # h2 = h
                    if h == 0:
                        ndp1[0] = (ndp1[0] + w0 * powq[0]) % MOD
                    else:
                        ndp0[h] = (ndp0[h] + w0 * powq[h]) % MOD
                if w1:
                    ndp1[h] = (ndp1[h] + w1 * powq[h]) % MOD
        else:
            for h in range(H+1):
                w0 = dp0[h]; w1 = dp1[h]
                # up
                h2 = h + alpha
                if h2 <= H:
                    factor = powq[h2]
                    if w1: ndp1[h2] = (ndp1[h2] + w1*factor) % MOD
                    if w0:
                        if h2 == 0: ndp1[0] = ... # h2 cannot be 0 if alpha>0 and h>=0? h+alpha>0, so no.
                        else: ndp0[h2] = ...
                # down
                if h >= alpha:
                    h2 = h - alpha
                    factor = powq[h2]
                    if w1: ndp1[h2] += w1*factor
                    if w0:
                        if h2 == 0:
                            ndp1[0] += w0*factor
                        else:
                            ndp0[h2] += w0*factor
        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
    return sum(dp1) % MOD

for h:
  if dp0[h]: ndp0[h] += dp0[h]*powq[h]
  if dp1[h]: ndp1[h] += dp1[h]*powq[h]

Let's see if there is any risk of double-counting due to different starting heights h representing same orientation? We argued no. Let's formalize: For a fixed orientation d_i, the valuation sequence is v_i = c + b_i where b_i is any particular solution. The set of all nonnegative solutions is c ≥ -min b. The one with min=0 is c=-min b. In DP, we sum over all nonnegative walks that touch 0. For this orientation, is there exactly one such walk? Suppose there are two different c values both yielding min=0? If c1<c2, min(v)=min(b)+c. If min=0 for both, then c=-min(b) unique. So yes. But could there be a nonnegative walk with min=0 that corresponds to the same orientation but with a different "starting height" because the solution space parameter c is not simply starting height? Starting height v_1 = b_1+c. If c unique, starting height unique. So no double count. Good.

Now, calc function:
def calc_prime(p, alphas, H):
    q = p % MOD
    powq = [1]*(H+1)
    for i in range(1,H+1):
        powq[i] = powq[i-1] * q % MOD
    dp0 = [0]*(H+1)
    dp1 = [0]*(H+1)
    for h in range(H+1):
        if h == 0:
            dp1[h] = 1
        else:
            dp0[h] = powq[h]
    for alpha in alphas:
        ndp0 = [0]*(H+1)
        ndp1 = [0]*(H+1)
        if alpha == 0:
            # for each h
            for h in range(H+1):
                w0 = dp0[h]
                if w0:
                    ndp0[h] = (ndp0[h] + w0 * powq[h]) % MOD
                w1 = dp1[h]
                if w1:
                    ndp1[h] = (ndp1[h] + w1 * powq[h]) % MOD
        else:
            for h in range(H+1):
                w0 = dp0[h]
                w1 = dp1[h]
                if w0 == 0 and w1 == 0:
                    continue
                # up
                h2 = h + alpha
                if h2 <= H:
                    factor = powq[h2]
                    if w1:
                        ndp1[h2] = (ndp1[h2] + w1 * factor) % MOD
                    if w0:
                        # h2 > 0
                        ndp0[h2] = (ndp0[h2] + w0 * factor) % MOD
                # down
                if h >= alpha:
                    h2 = h - alpha
                    factor = powq[h2]
                    if w1:
                        ndp1[h2] = (ndp1[h2] + w1 * factor) % MOD
                    if w0:
                        if h2 == 0:
                            ndp1[0] = (ndp1[0] + w0 * factor) % MOD
                        else:
                            ndp0[h2] = (ndp0[h2] + w0 * factor) % MOD
        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
    return sum(dp1) % MOD

Potential issue in alpha==0 case: If h=0 and dp0[0] maybe 0, but if w0 at h=0 somehow, h2=0 and touched should become true. Our code would add to ndp0[0], which would be wrong. But dp0[0] should always be 0. Let's prove invariant: dp0[h] represents touched_zero false. If h=0, then current vertex has valuation 0, so zero has been touched, so touched_zero must be true. Thus dp0[0]=0 always. Init yes. Transitions: w0 to h2=0 sets touched true, so goes to dp1[0], not dp0[0]. w0 to h2>0 stays dp0[h2]. w1 to any h2 stays dp1. So dp0[0] remains 0. Thus alpha==0 code safe. But to be robust, we can handle alpha==0 with same general logic:
for h:
  w0=dp0[h]; w1=dp1[h]
  h2=h
  factor=powq[h]
  if w1: ndp1[h]+=w1*factor
  if w0:
     if h2==0: ndp1[0]+=w0*factor
     else: ndp0[h]+=w0*factor
This is safer. But dp0[0]=0 anyway. We can write a helper to add transitions to avoid duplication? But for performance, inline.

Potential bug in factorization:
```
def factorize(a):
    d={}
    x=a
    for p in primes:
        if p*p > x:
            break
        if x % p == 0:
            cnt=0
            while x % p == 0:
                x//=p; cnt+=1
            d[p]=cnt
    if x > 1:
        d[x]=d.get(x,0)+1
    return d
```
If a=1, x=1, loop p=2: 4>1 break, x>1 false. Good.
If a=12, x=12, p=2: 4<=12, divide x=3, d[2]=2. Next p=3: p*p=9 > x=3 break, x>1 add d[3]=1. Good.
If a=49, x=49, p=2:4<=49 no divide; p=3:9<=49 no; p=5:25<=49 no; p=7:49<=49 divide x=1 d[7]=2; next p=11:121>1 break, x=1. Good.
If a=97 prime, p=2.. p=11? 11^2=121>97 break at p=11? Actually loop p=2,3,5,7 (49<=97), next p=11 (121>97) break, x=97 add. Good.

Now, calc_prime:
```
def calc_prime(p, alphas, H):
    q = p % MOD
    powq = [1]*(H+1)
    for i in range(1,H+1):
        powq[i] = powq[i-1] * q % MOD
    dp0 = [0]*(H+1)
    dp1 = [0]*(H+1)
    dp1[0] = 1
    for h in range(1,H+1):
        dp0[h] = powq[h]
    for a in alphas:
        ndp0 = [0]*(H+1)
        ndp1 = [0]*(H+1)
        if a == 0:
            for h in range(H+1):
                f = powq[h]
                w0 = dp0[h]
                if w0:
                    if h == 0:
                        ndp1[0] = (ndp1[0] + w0 * f) % MOD
                    else:
                        ndp0[h] = (ndp0[h] + w0 * f) % MOD
                w1 = dp1[h]
                if w1:
                    ndp1[h] = (ndp1[h] + w1 * f) % MOD
        else:
            for h in range(H+1):
                w0 = dp0[h]
                w1 = dp1[h]
                if not (w0 or w1):
                    continue
                h2 = h + a
                if h2 <= H:
                    f = powq[h2]
                    if w1:
                        ndp1[h2] = (ndp1[h2] + w1 * f) % MOD
                    if w0:
                        ndp0[h2] = (ndp0[h2] + w0 * f) % MOD
                h2 = h - a
                if h2 >= 0:
                    f = powq[h2]
                    if w1:
                        ndp1[h2] = (ndp1[h2] + w1 * f) % MOD
                    if w0:
                        if h2 == 0:
                            ndp1[0] = (ndp1[0] + w0 * f) % MOD
                        else:
                            ndp0[h2] = (ndp0[h2] + w0 * f) % MOD
        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
    return sum(dp1) % MOD
```
In alpha==0 branch, if h==0 and w0 nonzero, we add to ndp1[0]. But if w0 at h=0 impossible; safe. If h>0, h2=h>0, touched false remains. Good.

In alpha>0 branch, up transition h2 = h+a >0, so w0 stays ndp0. Down transition h2 = h-a can be 0. Good.

Potential issue: If a > H? impossible because a is one of alphas, H=sum alphas, so a≤H. But if H=0, calc not called. Good.

Now, let's think about if `powq[h2]` for h2 maybe not computed? h2≤H by cap. Good.

from the assistant.

The assistant has provided a long reasoning and is about to output final code. The final code should be in a code block. The reasoning seems correct. I will now produce the final code.

```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def sieve(n):
    is_prime = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_prime[0] = is_prime[1] = False
    primes = []
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            primes.append(i)
            for j in range(i * i, n + 1, i):
                is_prime[j] = False
    return primes

def factorize(x, primes):
    res = {}
    for p in primes:
        if p * p > x:
            break
        if x % p == 0:
            cnt = 0
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
                cnt += 1
            res[p] = cnt
    if x > 1:
        res[x] = res.get(x, 0) + 1
    return res

def calc_prime(p, alphas):
    H = sum(alphas)
    if H == 0:
        return 1

    q = p % MOD
    powq = [1] * (H + 1)
    for i in range(1, H + 1):
        powq[i] = powq[i - 1] * q % MOD

    # dp0: has not touched 0 yet, dp1: has touched 0
    dp0 = [0] * (H + 1)
    dp1 = [0] * (H + 1)
    dp1[0] = 1
    for h in range(1, H + 1):
        dp0[h] = powq[h]

    for a in alphas:
        ndp0 = [0] * (H + 1)
        ndp1 = [0] * (H + 1)

        if a == 0:
            for h in range(H + 1):
                f = powq[h]
                w0 = dp0[h]
                if w0:
                    if h == 0:
                        ndp1[0] = (ndp1[0] + w0 * f) % MOD
                    else:
                        ndp0[h] = (ndp0[h] + w0 * f) % MOD
                w1 = dp1[h]
                if w1:
                    ndp1[h] = (ndp1[h] + w1 * f) % MOD
        else:
            for h in range(H + 1):
                w0 = dp0[h]
                w1 = dp1[h]
                if not (w0 or w1):
                    continue

                h2 = h + a
                if h2 <= H:
                    f = powq[h2]
                    if w1:
                        ndp1[h2] = (ndp1[h2] + w1 * f) % MOD
                    if w0:
                        ndp0[h2] = (ndp0[h2] + w0 * f) % MOD

                h2 = h - a
                if h2 >= 0:
                    f = powq[h2]
                    if w1:
                        ndp1[h2] = (ndp1[h2] + w1 * f) % MOD
                    if w0:
                        if h2 == 0:
                            ndp1[0] = (ndp1[0] + w0 * f) % MOD
                        else:
                            ndp0[h2] = (ndp0[h2] + w0 * f) % MOD

        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

    return sum(dp1) % MOD

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]

    primes = sieve(1000)
    factors = [factorize(a, primes) for a in A]

    used_primes = set()
    for d in factors:
        used_primes.update(d.keys())

    ans = 1
    for p in used_primes:
        alphas = [d.get(p, 0) for d in factors]
        ans = ans * calc_prime(p, alphas) % MOD

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
